
## ideation
The problem asks to maximize the length of the shortest path from 1 to N after assigning weight 1 to exactly K edges (all others 0).  
- This is equivalent to **blocking** K edges so that every remaining 1‑N path has as many edges as possible.  
- For a candidate distance D, we need to know whether we can force all 1‑N paths to contain at least D edges of weight 1.  
- This is a classic **minimum s‑t cut** problem on a graph where each original edge is represented by a capacity‑1 edge. We build a node‑splitting construction: for each vertex v create v_in and v_out, connect v_in→v_out with capacity 1, and for each original edge u→v add an edge u_out→v_in with capacity INF (very large).  
- The min cut between 1_out and N_in equals the minimum number of original edges whose removal disconnects 1 from N.  
- If that min cut ≤ K, we can select those cut edges to set weight = 1 and make the shortest distance ≥ D.  
- Binary search D from 0 to M (since a path cannot have more edges than M). For each D, we need to check feasibility. ## worker: Implement the max flow construction for the D‑edge
We use binary search on the target distance D. The check for a fixed D is to determine whether we can select K edges so that every 1‑N path contains at least D of them. The D‑edge‑separator can be found by building a layered graph with D+1 copies of each vertex (indexed by the number of 1‑edges used so far) and adding a capacity‑1 edge from `u_i` to `v_{i+1}` for every original edge `u→v`. The source is vertex 1 at layer 0, the sink is vertex N at layer D. The max flow from source to sink in this layered graph equals the maximum number of edge‑disjoint paths that use at most D edges? Actually, by Menger's theorem, the min cut here corresponds to the minimum number of edges to hit every s‑t path in the original graph at least once in the layered structure, which effectively forces any s‑t path to take at least D steps in the layered graph, i.e., use at least D original edges. If the min cut value is ≤ K, we can select those cut edges as our 1‑edges and achieve distance ≥ D. We binary‑search the largest D satisfying this, and also cap D at the unweighted shortest path length L (because no path can have more edges than its length). Constraints N≤30, M≤100, D≤L≤29 keep the layered graph small (at most 30*30=900 nodes, 100*29≈2900 edges), so Dinic runs easily within limits.
